PREFACK.


oe


(s accordance with the instructions we received, our eim has been to render tne
“Preachers Homiletical Commentary” available as a Pulpit Encyclopedia. The
task as to size was somewhat formidable, and in several reapects presented special
diffsulties. The work to be dealt with consisted of twenty-six volumes, with
total of no less than 11,913 pages, each volume being oftentimes written by several
authors, upon widely different methods and in very varied styles.

Moreover, indexes to the separate volumes were not in all cases made, and when
in existence, were upon no uniform principle and not prepared with the same aim
as that of the present undertaking. But perhaps the real crux was how to index
sitles of a thoroughly homiletical type, when the whole point in these titles
tepended upon the passage from which they were deduced. As the work pro
seded, a few guiding principles had to be adopted, and we now briefly name
zome of them, as they may possibly assist in the use of the Index.

A mean—we trust a happy one—has been adopted between meagreness on
the one hand and repletion on the other.

The desire has been to index every really useful thought for Pulpit or Plat
form purpose, under all the words from which it would be likely to be sought
oy persons of differently constituted minds, or searching for information with
lifferent aims. In order not to defeat our object of aftording real help for ready
reference purposes, we avoided indexing every illustration or stray note or what
had evidently only a passing interest.

Every main fact, every cardinal doctrine, every moral duty, every philosophical
thought, every anecdote or telling illustration has been, we believe, faithfully
aoted and accurately indicated. There are about 3440 references to Main Topics
in the volume, and under such words as God and Sin, there are no less than 257
and 160 distinct Sub-headings respectively ; while the tetal number of the latter
amount to over 18,000.

The titles, too, had frequenuy in regard to the headings of the sermonie
sketches to be reduced in size, and reset, in order to make them fit into an index
which would be readily available and free from confusion.

Again, too, in every single instance we have endeavoured that the main
headings with all their sub-headings, should so read in the Tndex as to convey


vi PREFACE.


A Sg
a distinct and definite meaning. In some few cases, however, ths headings of the
writers were so peculiar that it was impossible either to make them read smoothly
or even convey a very definite idea apart from their context.

To avoid a needless multiplication of headings, the titles do not always read
in the same way. Thus the article, pronoun, or small word commaed off at the
end of a line may be read with and before either the sub-heading or the main
neading. We give ar example which will readily illustrate our meaning, and
also make it easy to enter into the spirit of the Index:—


Ohris= —


Red Heifer an Analogue of the, Th
Exessls as the Servant of God, Our


The Red Heifer an Analogue of the Christ.
Christ our Example as the Servant of God.


We may here mention that no little time and thought have been expended
with a view to making the headings symmetrical and uniform, so that the
arrangement might be analytical and suggestive.

Special efforts, also, have been made, both by the Publisher and Printers, that
the Typographical Arrangement should be as perfect as possible, and thus render
the use of the Index a pleasant and not a painful exercise. The introduction
of Clarendon Type for leading words and the adoption of a separate and unbroken
column for the references to the volumes are special features made in the interest
of the reader.

By following out these and similar principles of a scientific character, the
work of making the Index has been less of a drudgery to ourselves than other-
wise it would have been; and we trust the Index thus prepared will be helpful
and welcome to those who use it. May such constantly find it a clue to the
homiletical wealth which would otherwise be hopelessly hid in some five hundred
quires of paper, and in a huge bibliothecal forest of nearly ten millions of words
